JA - Mr. Emmerson
I consider that the third paragraph of the Soviet
FW
note of March l0 will be interpreted by the Soviet
Government to mean that they have maintained their
894.30/3-1047
position that the quadripartite commission, in
collaboration with SCAF, should be charged with the
division of the Japanese naval vessels.
I therefore suggest that consideration be given
to the advisability of adding a first paragraph to
our note which would not spell the matter out un-
necessarily but which would be of assistance to our
negotiators if the Russians do in fact take the
position I suggest. Such language might be deliberately
ambiguous and oan state that we acknowledge the receipt
of the soviet note in which they agree to our proposal
that they designate representatives in Japan to carry
out the arrangement proposed by this Government.

The Soviet Government accepts the proposal of the

representative with a group of six people accompanying

poration with the Supreme Commender of the Allied Powers
in Japen.

ships of the Japanese fleet of a tonnage higher than that
of destroyers, it would be grateful to the Government of
the U.S.A. for the transmission to it of such information.

=== Page 262 === (confidence 0.98)
一一
Unofficial translation of Note No. 14 -
The Embassy of the U.S.S.R. presents its compliments
to the Department of State of the U.S.A. and in conneotion
with the Department's notes of February 24 of this year
concerning the division of ships of the Japanese Navy of
the tonnage of destroyers and below has the honor to
state the following:
The Soviet Government accepts the proposal of the
Government of the U.S.A. to the effect that the division
of these ships should be entered upon without delay and
to such end will send to Japan in the near future its
representative with a group of six people accompa nying
him.
In connection with the distribution of the Japanese
ships of the tonnage of destroyers and below according
to grouns, and also the details of the organization of
the divis ion of the ships and the technicalities of
their delivery, in the opinion of the Soviet Government
all of these questions could be determ ned and agrzed
upon on the spot by representatives of the four Governments --
the U.S.S.R., U.S.A., Great Britain, and China in colla-
boration with the Supreme Command er of the Allied Powers
in Japan. The ships to be delivered to the Soviet Union
in accordance with the division must be sent to the port
of Nakhodka.
With reference to the fact that the Soviet Government
dees not possess information concerning the old war-
ships of the Japanese fleet of a tonnage higher than that
of destroyers, it would be grateful to the Government
of the U.S.A. for the transmission to it of such information.
Embassy of the Union of Soviet Soclalist Republics
Washington,
10 March 1947

CONFIDENTIAL
DRAFT NOTE TO BRITISH AMBASSADOR
The Acting Secretary of State presents his
compliments to His Excellency the British Ambassador and
has the honor to refer to his note of March 6, 1947, with
regard to the disposition of certain combatant vessels of
the Japanese Navy.
In reply to the question regarding the disposition
of Japanese naval tugs, harbor craft and salvage vessels,
it may be stated that these vessels are not regarded as
combatant vessels and have therefore not been included
in the list of combatant vessels of destroyer tonnage or
less to be divided among the United Kingdom, tne Union
of Soviet Socialist Republics, the Republic of China, and
the United States.
*
With regard to the request for certain technical
information concerning transports, minesweepers, and
auxiliary subchasers, it is understood that complete
information of this nature is already in the hands of the
British Admiralty. Shoula additional data be required,
it is suggested that inquiry be made of the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers in Tokyo by the appropriate
representatives of the British Government accredited
in Japan.
CONFIDENTIAL

The Detroit Free Press carried a Washington
date line dispatch Sunday as follows:
3
"The United States has informed Britain,
Russia and China that 239 Japanese
warships of destroyer size or less are
available for equal dvision among the
four Allied powers."

time to the effect that Russia pays no attention
to our request for settlement of lend lease ships.
If this is the case, why are any of these Japanese
warships turned over to Russia until at least
a settlement is made of the ships covered by lend
lease?

My dear Senator Vandenberg:
Reference is made to your memorandum of April 16,
1947 enclosing a communication from Mr. W. W. Mills of
894.30/4-1647
Detroit, Michigan on the subjest of the division of
certain vessels of the Japamese fleet in whion the sug-
gestion is made that the delivery of those ships to be
alloeated to the Soviet Umion be delayed until a settle-
ment is made by the Soviet Govermment for the ships
obtained from the United States through lend lease.
There is enclosed a copy of the Department's press
release of April ll deseribing the mature of the agree-
ment to divide certain Japanese combatant vessels among
the United States, United Kingdom, Chima and the Soviet
Union. It was agreed by these four Powers, following
the surrender of Japan, that all combatant vessels of
the Japanese fleet, with the exception of destroyers and
surface vessels of lesser tonnage, would be destroyed.
Those vessels in the category of destroyers or surface
vessels of lesser tonnage were to be divided equally
among the four Powers named.
In sorapping the Japanese vessels of destroyer ton-
nage and above, and in faoilitating the division of the
CS/A
remaining smaller combatant vessels, the Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers is acting on behalf of the Allied
Powers conoerned and not on behalf of the United States
Government alone. Im view of the international agreement
to divide these vessels and the fact that the United States
Government is mot aeting unilaterally in carrying out
894.30
such agreement
4-1647
The Henorable
Arthur H. Vandemburg,
United States Senete.

The following is the text of a note received from
the Britieh Ambassador dated April 22, 1947:
"His Majesty's Ambassador presents his compliments
to the Secretary of State, and with reference to Mr.
Marshall's communication of the 24th February, has the
honour to state that His Majesty's Government have taken
note of the proposed procedure for the division of
certain vessels of the Japanese Fleet.
"2. Whilst naming Singapore as the main port of
delivery His Majesty's Government do not wish to be
restricted to one port and they desire to reserve the
right to have part of the allocation sent to Hong Kong.
"3. His Majesty's Government would like the ships
to be accompanied by a repatriation ship for the Japanese
0
crews and they accept the conditions set out in the last
two sentences of paragraph (2) of the designated procedure.
A
"4. The Naval Adviser to the United Kingdom
Liaison Mission in Tokyo has been nominated to draw
lots on behalf of His Majestyts Government."

Secretary of State on April 11, 1947, dealing with the division

pursuant to the Loscow Declaration of October 30, 1943.
Ambassador Naggiar pointed out that the problem of the
disarmament of Japan had been under consideration by the Far Eastern

Committee No. 7 on disarmament of Japan. He requested that the
United States Representative on the Commission furnish him with in-
formation on this question, particularly as to the basis on which
the division of these vessels was made.

=== Page 287 === (confidence 0.98)
CONFIDENTIAL

TO
: JK - Mr. Hodge
DATE: May 2, 1947
es
FROM : JK - Mr. Baker
sUBject: Disposition of United States Government Share of ex-Japanese
Combatant Vessels.
nt Must Be Returned to
Attached is a copy of a proposed SWNCC paper which was discussed
8
394.30/5-247
at a meeting held in Comdr. Jurika's office at the Navy Department
and attended by Col.Jenkins and Maj. Miller of the War Department,
Comdr. Jurika and Comdr. Cresap of the Navy Department and H. L. Baker
of JK, State Department.
Inasmuch as the first 16 ships are now awaiting disposition in
Japan an early settlement of the problem is desirable. The total
U.S. share will probably be 60 vessels.
Representatives of the War and Navy Departments advanced the
following priority list for disposition of these ships.
1. Transfer of required vessels to French Government
2. Occupational use in Japan and Korea
C3/CVE

DRAFT (4/30/47)
SWNCC
THE PROBLEM
To determine the disposition of ex-Japanese combatant vessels which
are the share of the United States Government.
G
FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM
1. The first group of ex-Japanese combatant naval vessels are now
awaiting delivery at & port in Japan. The United States share of this
first grou is l6 shipe.
2. The ships will become the property of the U.S. Government and
plans for their eventual disposition must be made.
3. There is a shortage of steel scrap in the United States and through-
out the world. U.S. industrialists have approached various governmental
agencies and have requested that the United States share of ex-Japanese
vessels be returned to the United States and reduced to scrap.
4. The War Department, as the agency charg d with control over the
Japanese economy, is required to supply Japan's demands for scrap steel from
its current budget. If the ex-Japanese ships are turned over to the Japanose
7
for reduction to scrap in Japanese shipyards, there will be no exchange of
funds involved nor any cost to the American tax payer. This process will,
Confidential File
at the same time, provide employment for many Japanese.
5. Since the vessels accruing to the United States are, in fact,
former warships, they are not suitable for either use or conversion to
CONFTDTTTTAT

=== Page 289 === (confidence 0.96)
2
-2-
commercial purposes. The ships would be of no value to the oecupation
forces in the internal economy of Japan.

If it is determined by the SWNCC that these vessels, or a part of them,
are required for the Japanese economy, or for delivery to the United States
for reduction to scrap, the Navy is prepared to revise its requirements to
the minimum considered essential for purely naval purposes.
DISCUSSION
7. Although the ex-Japanese ships to be acquired by the United States
as a result of the division amongst China, Great Britain, the U.S.S.R. and
the United States become the property of the U.S. Government, the question
of their final disposition, i.e., reduction to scrap, use as target vessels,
etc., requires agreement between the executive agencies of the U.S. Govern-
ment. In order that the Naval authorities in Japan may plan for and be
prepared to accept delivery of these ships, their eventual disposition must
be decided. Since these vessels are excess to the requirements of the U.S.
Navy as fighting ships, and are completely demilitarized, the only use for
them would be as target vessels.
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 290 === (confidence 0.98)
-3
8. The ships are in perable condition in Japan, but the Navy does
not have the crews necessary to sail these ships to the United States.
Further, the Nevy has neither ep ropriations nor personnel to maintain
these ships indefinitely in Japanese waters while aweiting a decision a
to their eventual disposition. It is therefore imperative that the decision
as to their future use be reached short order.
CONCLUSIONS
9. It is ceneluded thet:
2. The U.S. Navy reeppraise its minimum re uirements of ex-Japanese
naval vessels to be used as targets.
b. U.S. industrialists he informed hy the Depertment of State that if
they desire ex-Japanese ships sailed to the U.S. for reduction to serap
that they be prepared to furnisn the necessary crews and pay voyage
expe,ses.
c. If U.s. industrialists refuse the offer in b above, the ships be
turned over to the Ja,enese for reduction to scrap in Japan.
RECOMMENDATIONS
10. It is recommended that:
a. Upon approval by the SWNCC, this paper be transmitted to the Joint
Chiefs of Staff for comment from the military point of view, and
b. Upon approval by the JCS, they forward a directive to SCAP apprising
him of the final disposition of the U.S. share of ex-Japanese naval
vessels.

=== Page 294 === (confidence 1.00)
CONFIDENTIAL
ENCLOSURE
DRAFT NOTE TO BRITISH AMBASSADOR
The Acting Secretary of State presents his compliments to
His Excellency the British Ambassador and has the honor to
refer to his note of March 6, 1947, with regard to the disposi-
tion of certain combatant vessels of the Japanese Navy.
In reply to the question regarding the disposition of
Japanese naval tugs, harbor craft and salvage vessels, it may
be stated that these vessels are not regarded as combatant
vessels and have therefore not been included in the list of
combatant vessels of destroyer tonnage or less, to be divided
among the United Kingdom, the Union of Soviet Socialist
Republics, the Republic of China, and the United States.
It is the intention of the U.S. Government that Japanese
ex-naval tugs, harbor craft and salvage vessels be used in the
economic rehabilitation of Japan in fulfillment of the objectives
of the occupation. Since the vast majority of these vessels
are of less than 1o0 gross tons, it is the position of the U.8.
Government that they are not subject to classification as
merchant vessels and hence are not available for reparations.
With regard to the request for certain technical infor-
mation concerning transports, minesweepers, and auxiliary sub
chasers, it is understood that complete information of this
nature is already in the hands of the British Admiralty. Should
additional data be required, it is suggested that inquiry be
made of the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers in Tokyo
by the appropriate representatives of the British Government
accredited in Japan.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
OFFICE
THE SECRETARY
DATE: May 13, 1947
1947 MAY 21 PM 4 21
SUBJECT:
Partition of the Japanese Fleet
894.30/5-1347
PARTICIPANTS: Mr. Makin, the Australian Ambassador
Mr. Stirling, the Australian Minister
The Secretary
Mr. Vincent, FE
COPIES TO:
U
A-H
EUR
NA
CA
1-1408
Ambassador Makin called at his request. He stated that he was
calling under instructions from his Government to bring to my
attention the feeling of his Government with regard to partition of
the Japanese fleet. He referred to recent information received
from the Dominion's Office in London to the effect that steps were
now being taken by General Macarthur to divide the Japanese fleet
CS/A
among the Chinese, Russians, British and Americans. He dwelt upon
the part played in the Pacific war by Australia and asserted that
Australia had a right to be counted in on the division of the
Japanese fleet. He said that his Government felt that any division
of the Japanese fleet should be deferred for consideration at a
conference on a Japanese peace treaty. In connection with the
treaty he commented with evident satisfaction on an article in
this morning's Washington Post which contained speculation that
the United States Government would be opposed to a five-power confer-
ence on a Japanese treaty and would favor an eleven-power conference
composed of the nations now represented in the Far Eastern Commission.
I told the Ambassador that as to the subject of the partition
894.
of the Japanese fleet, I would look into the matter.
Mr. Vincent reminded the Ambassador that agreement among the
1.30/5-1349
four powers regarding the Japanese fleet had been reached in the
autumn of 1945; that submarines and all naval vessels above the
NOF
size
24
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 300 === (confidence 0.98)
CONFIDENTIAL
-2-
size of destroyers were to be scrapped; and that destroyers
and surface vessels of lesser tonnage would be divided
equally among the four powers mentioned. He said that he
had assumed that the Australian Government had been informed
of this agreement, presumably by the Government of the
United Kingdom. The Ambassador said that his Government
had been informed but that it had not realized until it
received the information from the Dominion's Office that
steps were now being taken to turn over the vessels. He
repeated his previously expressed views, and the Secretary
again assured him that he would look into the matter.
There followed an unrelated casual conversation
with regard to methods of assuring security over the
telephone; development in the United States during the
war of the atomic bomb; the serious decisions that con-
fronted us at times during the war in Europe; and the
problem of eliminating the Japanese from the war without
having to drive them out of every bit of territory in
the Far East and Pacific which they occupied.
The call began at 11:30 and ended at 12 noon,
'ay 13, 1947.

=== Page 301 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
May 28, 1947
DATE:
Australian position with regard to division of
SUBJECT:
Japanese Navy.
Mr. Stirling, Minister, Australian Embassy
PARTICIPANTS:
General Hilldring
Mr. Vincent, FE
COPIES TO:
U
A-H
BUR
NA
CA
1-1403
Mr. Stirling called at 3 p.m. at General Hilldring's request.
The situation with regard to the division of the Japanese fleet was
reviewed. Mr. Stirling was reminded that the decision to divide the
fleet equally four ways among China, USSR, UK, and US was reached
something like a year and a half ago; that the principle upon which
the Australian Government based its request for reconsideration of
this matter at a peace treaty conference was fully appreciated by us;
that we had on a number of occasions indicated our sympathetie under-
standing of the Australian desire for full and equal participation in
war settlements in the Pacific; but that it was not practicable under
the cireumstances now to reopen the question of the division of the
Japanese Navy in as much as the agreement involved three other powers
and had been made so long ago.
In reply to an inquiry, Mr. Stirling said that he did not know
whether the Australian Government had taken up this matter with the
British Government. (Sir George Sansom and Mr. Graves of the British
Embassy had indicated to Mr. Vincent last week that they considered
the Australian proposal somewhat foolish and not worthy of serious
consideration.) In reply to another question, Mr. Stirling said that
he did not think the Australian Government had made a similar request
to the other two powers concerned; that is, UssR and China. Mr. Stirling
was informed that, in as much as the division was the subject of a
four-power agreement, concurrence in any modification would have to
be obtained from the other three powers. He was further informed
that it did not seem appropriate or practicable for the United States
Government

=== Page 302 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2
Government to take the initiative in suggesting modifi-
cation to the other three powers.
Summing up our position Mr. Stirling was informed
that, whereas we were sympathetic toward the prinoiple
that Australie deserved equal consideration in matters
pertaining to war settlements in the Pacific, Secretary
Marshall regretted that he could not accede to the
Australian Ambassador's request.
After some general and amicable conversation,
Mr. Stirling departed at 3:30 p.m., saying that he would
report our decision to his Government.

ORM NO. 64
Office Memorandum
THE SECRETARY
TO
: The Secretary
PARTMENT OF STATE
DATE: May 20, 1947
FROM : FE - Mr. Vincent
1947 MAY 20 PM 2 44
suBject: Visit of Australian Ambassador on May 13 With Regard
to the Division of the Japanese Fleet.
SECRET
The background of the problem of the disposition of Japa-
nese combatant vessels is as follows:
(1) The Navy originally desired to destroy all Japanese
naval combatant vessels. Following conversations between
Mr. Byrnes and Mr. Molotov in London in October 1945, Mr. Molotov
894.30/5-2047
informed Secretary Byrnes that "The Soviet Government agreed to
the destruction of submarines and surface vessels larger than
destroyers, but claimed one-fourth of the destroyers and vessels
of lesser tonnage".
(2) On October 26, 1945, the Chinese and British were in-
formed of the plan to destroy the larger vessels and divide the
smaller ones among the Four Powers and their representatives in
Washington expressed no objection.
(3) In accordance with a SWNCC decision of January 10, 1946,
the Department obtained the agreement of the U.K., USSR and China
to the division of the Japanese destroyers and surface combatant
vessels of lesser tonnage among the Four Powers when SCAP deter-
mined that they were no longer required for tasks connected with
V
the implementation of the Japanese surrender.
(4) SWNCC then determined the procedure for division of
these combatant vessels, all of which have been completely de-
militarized; the three Powers concerned were informed on Feb-
FILED
ruary 24, 1947 that certain vessels were ready for delivery,
that representatives should be designated in Tokyo to participate
in the drawing of lots and that ports of delivery should be spe
CS/A
ified.
(5) The U.K. and the USSR in reply to the Department's notes
have designated representatives in Tokyo and specified ports of
delivery. The Chinese Government has not yet replied formally
and the actual division of the ships in Tokyo now awaits only
8
the Chinese reply.
6
Recommendation:
i
It is recommended that Ambassador Makin be invited to
0
call upon General Hilldring and that General Hilldring (or if he
so desires, Mr. Vincent or Mr. Matthews) explain to the
Ambassador
SECRET

=== Page 313 === (confidence 0.98)
SECRET
-2-
Ambassador the background of the Four Power agreement to divide
certain combatant vessels of the Japanese Navy. The Ambassador
might be informed that decision to dispose of the Japanese fleet
after consultation among the USSR, U.K., China and the United
States was consistent with paragraph 2 of the Moscow Declaration
of November 1, 1943 which states that the U.S., U.K., USSR and
China "will act together in all matters relating to the surrender
and disarmament" of a comnon enemy. The Department has assumed
that Australia was informed by the United Kingdom of these arrange-
ments for the disposition of the Japanese fleet which were made
as long ago as October l945. In view of the long standing agree-
ment and the fact that all preparations have been completed to
proceed with the immediate division of the ships, the United States
Government coes not believe it would be practicable to seek a re-
vision of the Four Power agreement or to postpone settlement of
the problem until a peace conference.
JcV.
NA: JKEnkrson:mls
SECRET

=== Page 315 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: May 28, 1947
SUBJECT:
Australian position with regard to division of
Japanese Navy.
PARTiciPANTS: Mr. Stirling, Minister, Australian Embassy
89
General Hilldring
Mr. Vincent, FE
A-H
COPIES TO:
JUN 2
5-28
8
347
ero
1-1408
Mr. Stirling called at 3 p.m. at General Hilldring's request.
The situation with regard to the division of the Japanese fleet was
reviewed. Mr. Stirling was reminded that the decision to divide the
fleet equally four ways among China, USSR, UK, and US was reached
something like e year and a half ago; that the principle upon which
the Australian Government based its request for reconsideration of
this matter at a peace treaty conference was fully appreciated by us;
that we had on a number of occasions indicated our sympathetic under-
standing of the Australian desire for full and equal participation in
war settlements in the Pacific; but that it was not practicable under
the circumstances now to reopen the question of the division of the
Japanese Navy in as much as the agreement involved three other powers
and had been made so long ago.
In reply to an inquiry, Ir. Stirling said that he did not know
<
whether the Australian Government had taken up this matter with the
British Government. (Sir George Sansom and Mr. Graves of the British
Embassy had indicated to Mr. Vincent last week that they considered
the Australian proposal somewhat foolish and not worthy of serious
consideration.) In reply to another question, Mr. Stirling said that
he did not think the Australian Government had made a similar request
to the other two powers concerned; that is, USSR and China. Mr. Stirling
was informed that, in as much as the division was the subject of a
16
four-power agreement, concurrence in any modification would have to
be obtained from the other three powers. He was further informed
130/5-2847
that it did not seem appropriate or practicable for the United States
2
Government

=== Page 316 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
Government to take the initiative in suggesting modifi-
cation to the other three powers.
Summing up our position Mr. Stirling was informed
that, whereas we were sympathetic toward the principle
that Australia deserved equal consideration in matters
pertaining to war settlements in the Pacific, Secretary
Marshall regretted that he could not accede to the
Australian Ambassador's request.
After some general and amicable conversation,
ir. Stirling departed at 3:30 p.m., saying that he would
report our decision to his Government.
FE:JCVincent:bar

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
WESTERN
DIVISION OHEUROPEAN AFFAIRS
August 4, 1947.
RESTRICTED
MEMORANDUM
French Uncertainty as to Acceptance of Four
Former Jepanese Destroyers.
Both Miss Willis and I have made it clear to
the French that if they refuse the four former
Japanese destroyers they cannot use this refusal to
ask for other naval materiel. Paris, however, remains
doubtful of the value of these ships to the French
economy. The French Navy knows they are useless
for naval purposes. The Embassy here has been instruoted
to find out the last date on which they can make their
decision known. They want more time to explore the
possibility of using these vessels for civilian purposes.
8
6
The above was explained to me by Mr. Lacoste, Minister
94.30
Counselor, this morning.
I talked this matter over with Lt. Colonel Metz,
0
CP 35, Navy Depertment. General MacArthur has asked
that he have the French decision before the last drawing,
8
which takes place August 10. We agreed that I would ask
the French to have their answer here before the close of
business on Friday, August 8, so that it could be communicate
to Generel MecArthur, end that we would assume that if no
answer were received by that time that the French had decided
that they did not, repeat not, wish the four destroyers.
I then called Mr. Lacoste and reached the following
the close of business Friday, August 8. In case no answer
1s received by that time it will be assumed that the rench
Government decline the four destroyers. (Mr. Lacost
thought that the desdline was a rather short one, but agreed
that this was a metter too small on which to try General
MacArthur's patience any further.)
894
Copies to: Lt. Col.Metz, OP 35, Navy Dept.
NA
LE/M - Mr. Yingling
WE:WWallner:mdm
S

THE STATE-WAR-NAVY COORDINATING COMMITTEE
SWN-5575
22 July 1947
MEMORANDUM FOR H SECRETARY OF STATE:
Subject: SWNCC 186/27
247
Reference: SWNCC 186/28
894.30/7-2247
Enclosed herewith is a copy of SWNCC 186/27 as approved
by the State-War-Navy Coordinating Committee on 21 July 1947.
When the Attorney General has determined that the vessels
listed in paragraph 15 a (1) and (2) may be transferred to a
foreign government, it is requested that the Department of
State seek the agreement of the French and Chinese Governments
to the transfer of vessels specified in this report. Any
agreements made with the Governments of France or China rela-
tive to the transfer of ex-Japanese demilitarized combatant
vessels should contain provisions that the vessels are to be
accepted without future recourse to the United States Govern-
ment for any purpose whatsoever, including repairs, upkeep
and furnishing of adaitional equipage or spare parts. If the
Attorney General decides that such transfer cannot legally be
made, or the French or the Chinese Governments refuse the
ships offered to them, the State Department should inform
SWNCC and these ships will fall under provisions of priority
A/MJS
established in paragraph 15 e (5) and (5).
It is further requested that the Department of State P
advise the SWNco Secretariat of the results of the above
FILED
implementation so that the JCs may be instructed to advise::
21947
SCAP accordingly.
For the State-War-Navy Coordinating Committee:
J. H. HILLDRING
Chairman

SWNCC 186/27

CONFIDENTIAL
ENCLOSURE
DISPOSITION OF U.S. SHARE OF EX-JAPANESE COMBATANT VESSELS
THE PROBLEM
1. To determine the disposition of ex-Japanese combatant
vessels which are the share of the United States Government.
FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM
2. The Governments of the United Kingdom, China and the
U.S.S.R. have agreed to the terms and conditions set forth in
SWNCC 186/23 for the division and delivery of Japanese ex-
combatant vessels. The first group of these vessels are now
awaiting delivery.
3. The United States' share of this first group 1s 16
ships. In order that U. S. authorities in Japan may plan for
and be prepared to accept delivery of the U. S. share of
Japanese ex-combatant vessels, their final disposition must be
determined.
4. In a note from the French Ambassador to the Secretary
of State, dated January 8, 1946, it was stated inter alia:
"To compensate for the losses which she suffered in
both her Fleet and her base equipment, France feels
that she has a right to claim one surface vessel of
large tonnage, preferably an airplane carrier, and
four modern destroyers of from 2,100 to 2,300 tons.
She requests in addition the replacement of her de-
stroyed harbor equipment and the opportunity to use,
from now on by agreement with the Comman r-in-Chief
concerned, the Japanese naval material which is in
Indochina."
5. In the U.S. reply to the French note, dated March 7,
1946, there appeared inter alia:
"In view of the consideration mentioned in the
SWNCC 186/27
- 94 -
Enclosure

=== Page 341 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
Ambassador's notes under reference, this Government is
agreeable in principle to making suitable arrangements
for the transfer to the French Government of part of
the United States' share of Japanese surface vessels
of destroyer class and lesser tonnage, when the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers determines that they are
no longer needed for any task connected with the imple-
mentation of the Japanese surrender."
6. In a memorandum from the Secretary of State to the
President, dated August 14, 1946, the following statement
appeared:
"Finally, the 'Memorendum of Understanding between
the Government of the United States of America and the
Provisional Government of the French Republic Regarding
Settlement for Lend-Lease, Reciprocal Aid, Surplus War
Property, and Claims', initialed by representatives of
the two governments on May 28, 1946, contained the fol-
lowing provision:
'6 (i) The United States Government will
undertake to make available to the French Govern-
ment part of the United States Government's share
of captured German and Japanese surface naval ves-
sels when such vessels become excess to United
States needs and are no longer needed for any task
connected with the implementation of the German
and Japanese surrenders.'
"As indicated above, the Frenoh are aware that they
cannot expect to receive from us any submarines or any
surface naval vessels larger than destroyers. The remain-
ing vessels involved are few in number and, as indicated
in the memorandum addressed to you today by the Secretary
of the Navy, their transfer to the French Government would
be a matter of no strategio significance.
SWNCC 186/27
- 95-
Enclosure

=== Page 342 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
"Although this Government is committed to the general
principle of transferring to France a part of our share
of German and Japanese captured surface naval vessels,
the French understand that the details of the transfer
remain to be worked out and that there is no commitment
on the part of the United States Government to transfer
the vessels without compensation. In my opinion, however,
it is highly desirable for the following reasons that the
transfer be made without further compensation....
"In approving the so-called Leased Base-Destroyer
Deal with Great Britain, the Attorney General made it
clear that the compensation obtained for the vessels in
question did not have to be cash. In considering the
present problem he will doubtless wish to take into ao-
count the fact that this Government's undertaking to make
available to the French Government part of its share of
captured German and Japanese surface vessels was part of
a broad overall agreement with the French Government con-
taining a number of mutual concessions.
In the circum-
stances it might well be considered that although the com-
pensation received by the United States Government in this
instance is intangible, it is none the less real."
7. China has informally indicated that she desires the
entire U.S. share of Japanese ex-combatant ships, in addition
to her one-fourth share of the total allocable vessels.
8. There is a shortage of steel scrap in the United States
and throughout the world. U.s. industrialists have approached
various governmental agencies and have requested that the
United States' share of ex-Japanese vessels be returned to the
United States and reduced to scrap. However, it appears that
such a procedure would be uneconomical. The War Department,
as the agency charged with control over the Japanese economy, is

American taxpayer.

former varships, the majority are not suiteble for either use

CONCLUSIONS
15. It is concluded that:
a.

should be disposed of by CINCFE according to the following
priorities:
(1)
) Four destroyers to France, if they may
legally be transferred. If for any reason these
vessels are refused or cannot be transferred, they
fall into category (3) below.
. The French should be
informed that these vessels are completely demili-
tarized and refitting from Japanese sources is
impocsible.
(2) Iwo destroyors and three escorts to China
under the same terms as (1) above;
(3) Ships of high speed hull form (desi med and
powered for over 15 knots) ineluding destroyers, es-
corts, fast transports and fast minelayers:
(a) destroyer type vessels (destroyer
and/or destroyer escorts), 4 patrol vessels, 3
cubmarine chasers be accepted by the Conmander,
U.S. Naval Forces, Fer East, for use as targets
by the U.S. Navy.
(b) All other ships in this category be
considered as falling into category (5) below.
(4) Ships of low speed hull form:
(a) ships in this category which can be pro-
fitably employed in Japanese fishing and coastal
trade be returned to the Japanese Government under
current directives as surrendered military equip-

States in an account to be settled at some later
date with priority of reimbursement to be

No. 269
The French Embassy presents its compliments to the
Department of State and, with reference to the Embassy's
note dated July 10, 1947 concerning the division af the
Japanese fleet, has the honor to make the following
communication:
As the Embassy has already indicated to the Department
of State, the French naval authorities believe that the
Japanese vessels which were to be allocated to the French
Government could be rendered operable only after extensive
and costly alterati ons. On the other hand, the market value
of these vessels would not justify their dispatch to Franea
for industrial or commercial uses.
Consequently, the Prench Government feels obliged to
decline the kind offer of the American Government. It is
nonetheless desirous of expressing to the latter its thanks
for the understanding shown by the American authorities of the
needs in equipment of the French Navy which was so sorely
stricken during the war.
The French Embassy avails itself of the oocasion pre-
sented by this note to express once more to the Department
of State the assurances of its highest consideration.
Département d'Etat,
Washington, D. C.

CONFIDENTIAL
The Seoretary of State presents his compliments
to His Excelleney the Ambassador of the Union ef Soviet
Socialist Republics and has the honor to refer to the
previous agreement that all Japanese submarimes and all
Japanese combatant vessels larger than destroyers
should be destroyed but that destroyers and surface
combatant vessels of lesser tonnage should be divided
equally among Great Britain, the Union of Soviet
Socialist Republics, China and the United States. The
United States Government has been informed by the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers that certain
vessels of the Japanese fleet are now available for
division among the four powers mentioned above. The
Government of the Union of Soviet Socialist Republics
is therefore informed, as are the Governments of Great
Britain and hina in identic notes, of the following
procedure:
(1) Certain ex-Japanese navy ships within the
Japanese Empire and classified as "combatant and of
destroyer tonnage or less," are either wrecked, sunk
or require, with means available to the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers and without interfering
with the utilization of those means for projects assign-
ed a higher priority by th Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers, more than 60 days to place in operable
condition. Such remaining inoperable vessels will be
sorapped. A tentative list of these vessels is
enclosed, asEnclosure "A".
(2) It is intended that arrangements be made 1mme-
diately for the delivery with Japanese crews of those
vessels now deolared available and in good material and
operating condition by the SupremeCommander for the
Allied Powers, at such times and at such ports outside
of Japan as the receiving Governments may designate.
In order to facilitate the delivery, it is requested
that no port west of Singapore be designated and that
each Government designate no more than one port. An
accompanying vessel can be sent with each lot to
repatriate Japanese crews if the receiving nation does
not desire to assume responsibility for returning the
crews. The receiving Government should reimburse the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers in dollars or
in kind for all expenses involving expenditures of
United States

=== Page 354 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
United States supplies, such as fuel oil furaished these
vessels on the outward voyage, as well as fer fuel ail
consumed on the round trip or repatriation vessels used
to return the crews. The details of muoh arrangments
should be worked out between the Supreme Commander for
the Allied Powers and the representatives in Japan of
the Governments concerned.
(3) It is requested that your Government desig-
nate its representative in Japan to partieipate in the
drawing of lots to determine which of the four equiva-
lent lots of ships into whieh all allocable ships have
been divided will become the property of each of the
respective countries. The Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers will facilitate the inspeetion by repre-
sentatives of claimant nations of vessels prior te
their delivery. The Soviet Government is informed that
all ships have been demilitarized and items of military
equipment destroyed and that these ships ocannot be
remilitarized in Japan. A tentative list of the vessels
to be deli vered 1s enolosed as Enolosure "B".
(4) Delivery of additional vessels will be made as
soon as they become available. A tentative list or the
remaining vessels to be delivered is enclosed as
Enclosure "C".
(5) It should be noted that since each of the ten-
tative lists mentioned in thepreceding paragraphs was
prepared on 15 June 1946 the status of some of those
ships may in themsantime have been changed or may be
changed in the future due to damage or other exigencles.
(6) It will be noted from Enclosure "B" that
some sixty-four ships are now ready for delivery to the
four Governments concerned. It would therefore be
appreciated if the Soviet Government would inform the
United States Government promptly of the port in the
Far East to whieh it wishes its share of the ships to
be delivered. Upon receipt of this information, and
when the Soviet representative in Tokyo shall have
been empowered to act on behalf of his Government,
detailed plans for delivery will be carried out.
This communication is transmitted to the Soviet
Ambassador for his conridential information.

Department of State,

The Acting Secretary of State presents his compliments
to the Chargé d'Affaires ad interim of the Union of Soviet
Socialist Republics and acknowledges the receipt of the Em-
bassy's nete of March 10, 1947, accepting this Government's
proposal that the division of certain Japanese combat vessels
take place without delay end that a representative of the
Soviet Government be designated to participate in carrying out
the arrangements for the ditision of these ships as proposed
in the Department's notes of February 24, 1947.
Reference is made to the Embasay's statement thst the
Soviet Government does not possess information concerning those
warships of the Japanese fleet of a tonnage higher than that of
destroyers. In eccordance with the agreement of the Governments
of the United States, Union of Soviet Socialist Republios, Great
Britain and hina regarding the destruction of certain Japanese
naval vessels, the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
1ssued a directive to the Imperial Japanese Government on
April 30, 1946, (SCAPIN 910), providing that all former Japanese
surface combatant vessele within the Japanese Empire, readily
towable, larger than destroyers, and not employed by the oo-
cupation forces or in the repatriation services, were to be
destroyed by scrapping or other approved manner. A supplementary
direotive on this subject was issued to the Japanese Government
on June 6, 1946, (SCAPIN 1002).
The Soviet Chargé d'Afraires is informed that, in accordance
with these directives, former naval vessels of the Japanese Navy
falling within this category have been or are in the process of
being scrapped. As stated-in a letter addressed on May 5, 1946,
to Lieutenant General K. Derevyanko by Major General Paul J.
Mueller, Chief of Staff, General Headquarters, Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers, further detailed information on this sub-
ject may, if desired, be obtained by the Soviet Representative
in Tokyo from SCAP Headquarters.
Department of State,
Washington, April 25, 1947

His Majesty's Ambassador presents his compliments to
the Secretary of State and, with reference to Mr. Marshall's
communications of the 24th and 28th February relative to the
disposition of certain combstant vessels of the Japanese Navy,
has the honour to state that His Majesty's Government have

2.
Lord Inverchapel now wishes to enquire whether he may
be informed of the intentions of the United States Govern-
ment in regard to the disposal of Japanese naval tugs, harbour
craft and salvage vessels, none of which appeare to have been
included in the lists accompanying Mr. Marshall's note of the
24th February.
3.

gauge the usefulness and value of some of the vessels noted
on the Enclosure "C" of the note of the 24th February they
would be grateful if they could be supplied with the follow-
ing additional information:-
Transports. For each vessel:- size and speed and whether
reconverted to peace-time character.
Minesweepere. For each vessel:- type of hull and machinery,
size and speed.
Auxiliary Subchasers. For each vessel:- type of holl and
machinery, speed.

CONFIDENTIAL
The Secretary of State presents his compliments to His
Excellency the British Ambassador and has the honor to refer
to his note of March 6, 1947, with regard to the disposition
of certain combatant vessels of the Japanese Navy.
In reply to the question regarding the disposition of
Japanese naval tugs, harbor craft and salvage veesels, it may
be stated that these vessels ere not regarded as cembatant
vessels and have therefore not been included in the list of
combatant vessels of destroyer tonnage or less, to be divided
among the United Kingdom, the Union of Soviet Sociallst Re-
publics, the Republic of China, and the United States.
It is the intention of the United States Government that
Japanese ex-naval tugs, harbor craft and salvage vessels be
used in the economic rehabilitation of Japan in fulfillment of
the objectivee of the occupation. Since the vast majority of
these vessels are of less than 100 gross tons, it is the po-
sition of the United Statee Government that they are not sub-
ject to classificstion as merchent vessels anc hence are not
available for reparations.
With regerd to the request for certain teohnical informa-
tion concerning transports, minesweepers, and auxiliary sub-
chasers, it is understood that complete information of this
mature is already in the hands of the British Admiralty. Should
additional dsta be required, it 1s suggested that inguiry be
made of the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers in Tokyo by
the appropriate representativea of the British Government
accredited in Japan.
This communication 1s transmitted to the Ambassador for
his confidential informetion.
Department of State,
Washington, May 15, 1947.

CONFIDENTIAL
The Seoretary of State presents his compliments
to His Excelleney the Ambassador of the Union of Soviet
Socialist Republios and has the honor to refer to the
previous agreement that all Japanese submarines and all
Japanese combatant vessels larger than destroyers
should be destroyed but that destroyers and surface
combatant vessels or lesser tonnage should be divided
equally among Great Britain, the Union of Boviet
Socialist Republics, China and the United States. The
United States Government has been informed by the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers that certain
vessels of the Japanese fleet are now available for
division among the four powers mentioned above. The
Government of the Union of Soviet Socialist Republics
is therefore informed, as are the Governments of Great
Britain and hina in identic notes, of the follewing
procedure:
(1) Certain ex-Japanese navy ships within the
Japanese Empire and classified as "combatant and of
destroyer tonnage or less," are either wrecked, sunk
or require, with means available to the Suprems
Commender for the Allied Powers and without interfering
with the utilization of those means for projects assign-
ed a higher priority by th Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers, more than 60 days to place in operable
condition. Such remaining 1noperable vessels will be
scrapped. A tentative list of these vessels 1s
enclosed, asEnolosure "A".
(2) It is intended that arrangements be made imme-
diately for the delivery with Japanese crews of those
vessels now deolared available and in good material and
operating condition by the SupremeCommander for the
Allied Powers, at such times and at such ports outside
of Japan as the receiving Governments may designate.
In order to facilitate the delivery, it is requested
that no port west of Singapore be designated and that
each Government designate no more than one port. An
accompanying vewsel can be sent with each lot to
repatriate Japanese crews if the receiving nation does
not desire to assume responsibility for returning the
crews. The receiving Government should reimburse the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers in dollars or
in kind for all expenses involving expenditures of
Unitec States

=== Page 364 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
United States supplies, such as fuel oil furnished these
vessels on the outward voyage, as well as for fuel oil
consumed on the round trip of repatriation vessels used
to return the crews. The details of muoh arrangments
should be worked out between the Supreme Commander for
the Allied Powers and the representatives in Japan of
the Governments concerned.
(3) It is requested that your Government desig-
nate its representative in Japan to partioipate in the
drawing or lots to determine which of the four equiva-
lent lots of ships into whieh all allocable ships have
been divided will become the property of each of the
respective countries. The Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers will facilitate the inspeetion by repre-
sentatives of claimant nations of vessels prior to
their delivery. The Soviet Government is informed that
all ships have been demilitarized and items of military
equipment destroyed and that these ships cannot be
remilitarized in Japan. A tentative list or the vessels
to be del1 vered 1s enclosed as Enolosure "B".
(4) Delivery of additional vessels will be made as
soon as they become avallable. A tentative list of the
remaining vessels to be delivered is enolosed as
Enclosure "C".
(5) It should be noted that sinoe each of the ten-
tative lists mentioned in thepreceding paragraphs was
prepared on 15 June 1946 the status of some of these
ships may in themeantime have been changed or may be
changed in the future due to damage or other exigencies.
(6) It will be noted from Enelosure "B" that
some sixty-four ships are now ready for delivery to the
four Governments concerned. It would therefore be
appreciated if the Soviet Government would inform the
United States Government promptly of the port in the
Far East to whieh it wishes its share of the ships to
be delivered. Upon receipt of this information, and
when the Soviet representative in Tokyo shall have
been empowered to act on behalf of his Government,
deteiled plans for delivery will be carried out.
This communication is transmitted to the Soviet
Ambassador for his confidential information.
Enclosures:
"A", "B", and "C"
Department of State,
